# How the IRS Processes Free-Text Explanations: An IRM Map

This document takes each of the 1,210 free-text explanations in *List of Free-Text Explanations IRS Requires or Requests.md* and records what the Internal Revenue Manual (IRM) says about how the IRS receives, routes, reviews, or acts on that explanation. The IRM text reviewed is the version published on irs.gov as of October 30, 2026: 1,479 sections across Parts 1, 3–5, 7–9, 11, 13, 20–22, 25, and 30–39, with the remaining Parts 2, 6, and 10 (298 more sections, including the IRS AI governance and generative-AI policy) added for the AI analysis. Items keep the numbering and grouping of the source list. Prepared with the help of Claude.

**Coverage:** The IRM gives direct guidance for 296 explanations and related guidance for 473. For 50, only a general rule applies; for 391, I found nothing.

**NLP/AI processing contemplated by the IRM: 0 of 1,210 explanations.** No IRM provision that governs any explanation on the list says to process it with natural-language processing, machine learning, or other AI. Wherever the IRM addresses handling, an IRS employee does the reading. This holds across the entire public IRM (all 1,777 sections in all Parts, including Part 10's IRS Policy for Artificial Intelligence (AI) Governance, IRM 10.24.1, and its Generative AI (GenAI) Policy, IRM 10.24.1.7). That policy sets general rules for any IRS AI use but does not identify any use for reading taxpayer explanations (see finding 10). Search terms included "artificial intelligence," "machine learning," "natural language," "NLP," "large language model," "generative," "text mining," "OCR," and "keyword search."

- **Direct** = an IRM passage tells IRS employees what to do with this explanation, or with a narrow class of explanations that includes it.
- **Related** = the IRM addresses the form, line, or program, but not how to handle the explanation itself.
- **General only** = only a general rule applies, such as the penalty-relief framework.
- **None found** = no IRM passage addresses processing of the explanation.

| Group (from the source list) | Items | Direct | Related | General only | None found |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| Mandatory Free-Text Attachments | 656 | 90 | 200 | 50 | 316 |
| Mandatory Free-Text in Form, Attachment if Long | 517 | 201 | 249 | 0 | 67 |
| Mandatory Free-Text in Form, Attachment Not Contemplated | 10 | 3 | 6 | 0 | 1 |
| Optional Free-Text Attachments | 14 | 1 | 7 | 0 | 6 |
| Optional Free-Text in Form, Attachment if Long | 13 | 1 | 11 | 0 | 1 |

## Key findings

1. **Many explanations are never addressed in the IRM.** For 391 of the 1,210 explanations, no IRM passage addresses how the explanation is processed. Another 50 fall only under a general rule. The gap is largest for statements required by Treasury regulations and revenue procedures, which the IRM rarely cites, and for supplemental statements attached to business returns and international forms.
2. **Narratives are read in earnest mainly in a few determination programs.** These programs account for most of the "Direct" in-form narratives:
   - *EO Determinations (Forms 1023, 1024, 1028, 8940).* Specialists are told that "the organization must describe its activities in sufficient detail to permit you to conclude that it will meet the requirements of its requested subsection." But they "only ask activity development questions if there's an indication that the activities/purposes are potentially contrary to the requirements for recognition of tax exemption" ([7.20.2.3](https://www.irs.gov/irm/part7/irm_07-020-002r)).
   - *The SS-8 program.* "An SS-8 technician applies the law to the facts and circumstances submitted" ([7.50.1.1.1](https://www.irs.gov/irm/part7/irm_07-050-001r)).
   - *Innocent-spouse examiners (Form 8857 knowledge, involvement, and abuse narratives).* See [25.15.3.11.1](https://www.irs.gov/irm/part25/irm_25-015-003r).
   - *VCP specialists (Form 8950 and model compliance statements).* See [7.2.2.7](https://www.irs.gov/irm/part7/irm_07-002-002).
3. **At intake, the IRS mostly checks whether an explanation is there, not what it says.** For Form 990, the Incomplete Return Item codes flag a return where "Schedule O, Supplemental Information to Form 990, is missing or blank" ([21.7.7.6.23.1.3.1](https://www.irs.gov/irm/part21/irm_21-007-007r)). No IRM passage directs review of the content of the Schedule O governance or program descriptions. Other examples: Form 8283 without a "supporting statement" triggers correspondence ([3.11.3.20.1.7](https://www.irs.gov/irm/part3/irm_03-011-003r)), as does an amended return with "no explanation of the change" ([3.11.6.12.1](https://www.irs.gov/irm/part3/irm_03-011-006r)). Code & Edit treats attachments as data to "edit/transfer" onto form lines ([3.21.19.12](https://www.irs.gov/irm/part3/irm_03-021-019r)).
4. **Sometimes the explanation decides the outcome, and sometimes it is ignored.** On employment-tax corrections, a Form 94X-X filed after year-end to reduce withheld income tax is disallowed if "[t]he explanation section indicates the correction requested is not for an administrative error." Yet increases are accepted "whether or not a Form 941-X, supporting statement, or explanation is attached" ([21.7.2.4.4.3](https://www.irs.gov/irm/part21/irm_21-007-002r), [3.11.13.14.21](https://www.irs.gov/irm/part3/irm_03-011-013r)). On the corporate AMT form, if "[t]he taxpayer attaches a worksheet or an explanation for an adjustment to Line 1," Error Resolution accepts the taxpayer's figure ([3.12.251.32.36](https://www.irs.gov/irm/part3/irm_03-012-251r)).
5. **Reasonable-cause statements attached to returns are deliberately not evaluated when the return is processed.** Code & Edit is told: "Do not edit CCC "R" when a reason is given for the delay in filing a return." Instead it sends Letter 1382C ([3.11.16.9.1.18](https://www.irs.gov/irm/part3/irm_03-011-016r)). "Code and Edit examiners are no longer responsible to make penalty or interest determinations on taxpayer requests, except for returns indicating a disaster" ([3.12.23.5.5](https://www.irs.gov/irm/part3/irm_03-012-023r)). Such statements are weighed later under IRM 20.1.1.3, where "the dates and explanations should clearly correspond with events on which the penalties are based" ([20.1.1.3.2.2](https://www.irs.gov/irm/part20/irm_20-001-001r)). There are exceptions:
   - Form 5330 (Code & Edit edits CCC "R").
   - Form 706-series and Form 708 returns, where Cincinnati "will make all determinations of reasonable cause" ([3.11.106.30.12.8](https://www.irs.gov/irm/part3/irm_03-011-106r)).
   - Form 4768 extension requests, where employees "evaluate reasonable cause statements" ([5.5.5.5](https://www.irs.gov/irm/part5/irm_05-005-005)).
6. **When explanations are evaluated, the standard is often generous.** For late S-corporation elections: "If taxpayer provides a statement acknowledging late filing with any plausible reason, and has included all other statements, give the taxpayer the benefit of the doubt" ([3.13.2.23.4](https://www.irs.gov/irm/part3/irm_03-013-002r)). For late tax-exempt-bond returns, CP 143 "informs the issuer their reasonable cause explanation for late filing was accepted" once a reply posts, unless the issuer has a late-filing history ([21.7.7.7.4.4.3](https://www.irs.gov/irm/part21/irm_21-007-007r)). A Form 8868 extension for Form 5330 "is approved if it is timely, signed, and has an acceptable reason for the extension" ([3.11.20.11.15.2](https://www.irs.gov/irm/part3/irm_03-011-020r)).
7. **Examination uses disclosures as risk signals.** OTSA "collects, reviews, and maintains a database" for Schedule UTP and for Forms 8275/8275-R ([4.50.5.2.4](https://www.irs.gov/irm/part4/irm_04-050-005r), [4.50.5.2.1](https://www.irs.gov/irm/part4/irm_04-050-005r)). A disclosure is adequate if it "reasonably apprises the IRS of the nature and amount of the potential controversy" ([20.1.5.8.2.1](https://www.irs.gov/irm/part20/irm_20-001-005)). Country-by-country reports feed transfer-pricing risk assessment ([4.61.3.3.8](https://www.irs.gov/irm/part4/irm_04-061-003)).
8. **The public IRM is incomplete in places.** Several relevant passages contain redactions (shown as "≡ ≡ ≡" on irs.gov), including parts of 3.13.2.23.4 (late S elections) and 21.7.7.6.16.6 (Chapter 42 abatements). The public text may not show every criterion employees apply.
9. **No IRM provision contemplates NLP or AI processing of any explanation on the list (0 of 1,210).** The nearest provisions are mechanical: employees can search scanned employee-plans and exempt-organization case files "for words or phrases using Optical Character Recognition (OCR)" ([7.15.6.10](https://www.irs.gov/irm/part7/irm_07-015-006)); data from "various fields" of Forms 5300, 5307, and 5310 is transcribed manually or "using optical character recognition" ([7.15.1.7.2](https://www.irs.gov/irm/part7/irm_07-015-001)); and Schedule UTP data fields are extracted into a database that OTSA staff review and categorize ([4.50.5.2.4](https://www.irs.gov/irm/part4/irm_04-050-005r)). Where the IRM does mention AI or machine learning, it is for selecting returns for audit from structured data (e.g., "Issue Recommender," [4.1.5.3.1](https://www.irs.gov/irm/part4/irm_04-001-005); [4.50.2.2.2](https://www.irs.gov/irm/part4/irm_04-050-002r)), organizational mission statements, phone systems, and drafting the IRM itself ([1.11.2.10](https://www.irs.gov/irm/part1/irm_01-011-002)), never for reading taxpayer explanations.
10. **The IRS's AI governance and generative-AI policy (IRM 10.24.1) sets general rules for AI use but authorizes no particular use on taxpayer explanations.** IRM 10.24.1 (revised August 10, 2026; owned by the Chief Data and Analytics Officer) governs AI "by use cases," which business units must record in an internal AI Use Case Inventory. The inventory itself is not in the IRM, so the public IRM cannot show whether any use case reads free-text explanations ([10.24.1.3](https://www.irs.gov/irm/part10/irm_10-024-001r), [10.24.1.5](https://www.irs.gov/irm/part10/irm_10-024-001r)). Provisions that would bear on AI processing of explanations:
   - *High-impact AI.* AI is "high-impact" when its output "serves as a principal basis for decisions or actions that have a legal, material, binding, or significant effect" on rights or access to government services. Uses presumed high-impact include "AI that informs or influences whether a taxpayer will be subject to audit, or what aspects of a return will be subject to audit" ([10.24.1.4.1](https://www.irs.gov/irm/part10/irm_10-024-001r), [10.24.1.4.2](https://www.irs.gov/irm/part10/irm_10-024-001r)).
   - *Safeguards for high-impact AI.* High-impact uses require pre-deployment testing, an AI impact assessment, ongoing monitoring, and "additional human oversight, intervention, and accountability." They must also "[o]ffer consistent remedies or appeals" ([10.24.1.4.3](https://www.irs.gov/irm/part10/irm_10-024-001r)).
   - *Generative AI.* Employees may not "[u]se any outputs from AI systems in the performance of government business without ensuring direct human review to confirm the veracity of the information." They must not "use GenAI to make binding determinations or actions on taxpayer rights without proper oversight," or "generate taxpayer-facing communications without appropriate validation" ([10.24.1.7.2](https://www.irs.gov/irm/part10/irm_10-024-001r), [10.24.1.7.3](https://www.irs.gov/irm/part10/irm_10-024-001r)).
   - *Taxpayer rights.* "IRS use of AI must not violate these rights" ([10.24.1.9](https://www.irs.gov/irm/part10/irm_10-024-001r)).
   - *Privacy.* The privacy IRM lists "Natural language processing (NLP)" among the AI-associated technologies its policy covers ([10.5.1.6.22](https://www.irs.gov/irm/part10/irm_10-005-001)). AI systems using taxpayer data need a privacy and civil liberties impact assessment at least 30 business days before deployment ([10.5.2.2.13](https://www.irs.gov/irm/part10/irm_10-005-002)).

   Elsewhere in the newly added Parts, NLP appears only in IT standards describing keyword-recognition chatbots ([2.5.3](https://www.irs.gov/irm/part2/irm_02-005-003)). A 2004 provision mentions a "Correspondex Expert System" from the IRS "Artificial Intelligence Lab" that helps employees create and review outgoing IRS letters ([2.4.6.2.7](https://www.irs.gov/irm/part2/irm_02-004-006)). Neither involves reading taxpayer explanations. *Interpretation (not stated in the IRM):* If the IRS used AI to evaluate explanations that drive penalty relief, elections, claims, or audit selection, those uses would likely be presumed or determined high-impact. They would then trigger the safeguards above.

*Abbreviations in the last column:* AM = Accounts Management; AUR/BUR = Automated/BMF Underreporter; C&E = Code and Edit; CCC = Computer Condition Code; CCISO = Cincinnati Centralized Innocent Spouse Operation; EOCU = Exempt Organizations Compliance Unit; ERS = Error Resolution System; HFA = housing finance agency; IRI = Incomplete Return Item; MeF = Modernized e-File; OTSA = Office of Tax Shelter Analysis; RC = reasonable cause; RPC = Return Processing Code; SP = Submission Processing; TAC = Taxpayer Assistance Center; TE = tax examiner; "->" = results in. IRM links go to the chapter page on irs.gov; search the page for the subsection number.


**Prepared with the Help of Claude**

## Method and limitations

- **Corpus.** All 1,479 IRM sections listed on irs.gov for Parts 1, 3, 4, 5, 7, 8, 9, 11, 13, 20, 21, 22, 25, and 30–39 were downloaded on October 30, 2026, and were used for the item-by-item analysis. On November 7, 2026, the remaining Parts 2, 6, and 10 (298 sections, including IRM 10.24.1, IRS Policy for Artificial Intelligence (AI) Governance) were added. That brings the corpus to all 1,777 sections of the public IRM. The added Parts were searched for AI and NLP terms and for explanation-handling terms; they contained no provisions on processing explanations on the list, so the item-by-item results did not change.
- **Search.** Each explanation was searched by form number, schedule, regulation or Code section, or revenue procedure, combined with explanation-related terms (explanation, statement, narrative, describe, reasonable cause) and item-specific concepts. Hits were reviewed by hand, and IRM guidance was assigned at the item level or form level. Where an item matched one already analyzed in an earlier pass, that analysis was reused.
- **Verification.** Every quotation in this document was checked by machine against the downloaded IRM text, and every cited IRM subsection was confirmed to exist.
- **"None found" is not proof of absence.** Search terms can miss passages, and some handling is described outside the IRM: revenue procedures (e.g., Rev. Proc. 2026-1 for letter rulings), job aids, desk guides, LB&I practice units, and redacted IRM text. Form-level assignments may overstate coverage for an individual line.
- **Snapshot.** The IRM changes frequently through interim guidance; check the current version before relying on any passage.
